This person is referred to as a 'proxy'; see arts 13.6 - 13.9 of Appendix 1B and arts 45 and 46 of Appendix 1C. If a director is unable to attend a board meeting, they may be able to appoint someone to represent them. This person is referred to as an 'alternate'; see art.20.16 of Appendix 1B and art.16(1) of Appendix 1C. A proxy may be an 'open proxy' which confers a discretion upon the proxy on how to vote either for or against a motion; it may be a 'specific proxy' in which case the proxy must vote in accordance with the directions of the person appointing him. Whereas an alternate director must act independently and decide personally how to vote on a particular issue (although they may have regard to the known views of the director appointing them).
6. The chairman of a meeting is usually required to confirm that the meeting has been properly convened and that a quorum is present, to lead discussions and preserve order, to make sure the required business is covered and to ensure that decisions are properly recorded.
7. (a) Voting at a general meeting of the shareholders is initially by a 'show of hands' , which means one vote per shareholder present and proxy votes are not counted. But, if a sufficient number of members so request, votes are counted by a 'poll' , which means one vote per share owned and proxy votes are counted.
4. (b) Voting at a meeting of the directors is by a show of hands and resolutions are decided by a simple majority.
8. There are no prescribed requirements as to how minutes of a meeting should be structured although each service provider will have their own set of precedents which should be followed and adapted where appropriate. The conventional format of minutes of meetings is illustrated throughout the Appendixes of the online study resources.
9. The minutes of a meeting of directors usually comprise:
- confirmation of the date and place of the meeting
- persons present and those in attendance
- persons absent and apologies
- appointment of a chairman (and sometimes a secretary) of the meeting
- confirmation that adequate notice has been issued and the meeting properly convened
- confirmation that a quorum is present
- the business conducted, usually following the notice of the meeting and including details of the resolutions passed
- the date of the next meeting, if applicable
- note of the termination of the meeting.
10. The types of resolution required are:
- a. a directors' resolution is sufficient to fill a casual vacancy until the next AGM; permanent board members are appointed, subject to retirement by rotation every three years, by ordinary resolution of shareholders
- b. special resolution of the members

- c. directors' resolution usually required, although individual members with enough shares can also instruct the board or company secretary to convene a general meeting
- d. directors' resolution.
11. When there has been a meeting (of shareholders or directors) and resolutions passed, it is appropriate to record the resolutions in a minute of the meeting. When a formal meeting has been dispensed with under the provisions of typical articles, a formal minute is inappropriate. Instead, a 'written resolution' should be drafted, signed and recorded. The conventional format of written resolutions is illustrated throughout the Appendixes of the online study resources.
12. a. See section 5.2.
- b. See section 5.3.
- c. See section 5.6.
- d. See section 5.8.
- e. See section 5.10.

## Module 11:

## Answers to self-assessment questions

1. Banks; trust companies; independent corporate services providers; law firms; accounting firms, all of whom must hold a license from the local regulator permitting them to conduct corporate management services in and from their local jurisdiction. See section 1.
2. This depends on the firm and the centre chosen, although the following services are usually provided:
- incorporation of a company
- registration and continuance
- provision of directors and officers
- provision of a registered office
- provision of a registered agent
- provision of nominee shareholders
- maintenance of corporate records
- statutory filings and secretarial services
- accounting services.
3. The corporate service provider provides the registered 'nominee' shareholder to whom shares of a managed company are issued. The nominee shareholder is often a corporate subsidiary of the service provider. The directors of the nominee shareholder then execute a nominee shareholder agreement or declaration confirming on whose behalf the shares are held - see Appendix 27A and 27B of the online Study Resources.
4. The nominee shareholder promises: to hold the shares issued by the managed company as nominee or agent for and on behalf of the beneficial owner; not to sell, transfer, mortgage or dispose of such shares unless instructed to do so; to vote the shares as instructed; to assign any dividends that are distributed by the company to the beneficial owner.
5. The shares of most offshore companies are not registered in the name of the beneficial owner (client) but instead registered in the name of a nominee, (which is usually a company owned and operated by the corporate service provider). The beneficial ownership of the shares is recorded by the nominee executing a declaration which usually confirms that the shares are held by the nominee as nominee or agent (or 'bare trustee') for the beneficial owner. In some cases the nominee declaration may specify that the shares are held for a named beneficial owner and that on the death of that person the shares will be held for other named beneficial owners. This is intended to clarify who is to become the new beneficial owner of the company on the death of the first/original beneficial owner and to prevent possible probate problems on the death of the beneficial owner. However, it should be noted that such an arrangement is a testamentary gift which could result in the service provider being held liable for

intermeddling in the estate of the beneficial owner and/or committing a fraud on the estate. One way of avoiding the problem would be for the shares to be registered in the name of a nominee and for the nominee to hold the shares for and on behalf of the trustees of an offshore trust created to act as the holding entity.

6. (a) The shares in AB Inc are held as nominee for the survivor, Orange, as, when held jointly , the share of a deceased co-owner automatically passes to the surviving co-owner.
2. (b) If the nominee declaration stated that the shares were held 'as nominee for Green and Orange in common ' , the interest in the shares of the deceased co-owner will now pass to the heirs and estate of Green according to the applicable laws of succession - so the nominee now hold a one-half interest in the shares for the heirs and estate of Green and the remaining one-half interest for Orange, equally.
7. The possible problems associated with management and control being held by the client beneficial owner include:
- adverse tax consequences insofar as the company may become liable to pay corporation tax in the jurisdiction from where the business of the company is managed and controlled
- loss of confidentiality.
8. There is a risk that the company will suffer loss by following poor advice given by the client beneficial owner. Liability for such loss could be traced back to the directors. It is therefore essential:
- that the directors ensure that the client is competent to give investment advice
- that shareholder approval is obtained to confirm the appointment
- that suitable exoneration and indemnity provisions are contained in the articles or the directors' service agreement (DSA); note however that such clauses do not exonerate the directors from liability in all jurisdictions nor for bad faith or wilful default.
9. (a) The usual response would be to discourage the grant of a general power of attorney, because of the problems associated with giving a third party power to enter into transactions that bind the company, the terms of which the service provider would not necessarily be aware. This could incur criminal liability under AML and CTF legislation. In addition, there are adverse tax consequences associated with giving the beneficial owner power of management and control, causing the company to become tax resident onshore.
11. (b) A general power of attorney confers upon the attorney-in-fact an unrestricted general power to represent the company and to enter into binding deals, contracts and transactions on its behalf. Whereas, a special power of attorney confers upon the attorney-in-fact a restrictive power, curtailed in terms of time and to specified transaction. For example, a special power of attorney may confer a power for a limited duration of up to three months which may be exercised only to bind the company into identified types of contracts or transaction or up to a maximum financial exposure.

10. The directors should not proceed. Alternatively, they should consider obtaining shareholder approval following full disclosure of the risks. The service provider may also wish to consider the advisability of continuing with the business relationship. It is also possible that the service provider may be under a duty to report the matter to the local money laundering authority if there exists a reasonable suspicion of criminal misconduct.

## Module 12:

## Answers to self-assessment questions

1. Whenever creditors of a company realise that the company is in financial difficulties and may be approaching insolvency they have several alternative options beyond simply suing for recovery:
2. (i) if a creditor has security, they can appoint a receiver (section 1.1)
3. (ii) they could induce the board to propose a compromise arrangement (section 1.2)
4. (iii) they could apply to the court to appoint an administrator in certain jurisdictions where this is available, such as BVI, Guernsey and Mauritius (section 1.3).
5. 2 A fixed charge is a legal mortgage over specified assets, usually real estate, which essentially allows the company to retain possession and use of the secured property but precludes the company from dealing with such property while the mortgage subsists. A floating charge is an equitable charge on some or all of the company's present and future movable assets, which permits the company not only to retain possession and use of such assets, but also the right to sell or deal with the secured assets in the ordinary course of business. On the occurrence of an act of default (for the fixed charge) or crystallising event (for the floating charge), the secured creditor will typically appoint a receiver and manager over the secured assets.
3. The receiver will take control of the mortgaged assets (which will typically consist of all the company's real and personal property) and determine whether the business is viable. If it is, the receiver will run the business and apply any profit to pay down the debt due to the secured creditor. If the business is not viable, the receiver will sell the business as a going concern to a willing buyer, or simply sell the assets piecemeal for what they will fetch, applying the proceeds to pay down the debt due to the secured creditor. The surplus, if any, belongs to the company and is available to the ordinary unsecured creditors.
4. The usual grounds are that:
- the company is insolvent and cannot pay its debts
- the court believes it would be just and equitable to wind the company up.
5. The typical procedure is:
11. (a) a creditor applies to court, citing one of the statutory grounds, usually that the debtor company is insolvent
12. (b) an early hearing takes place where, unless the company can prove it is solvent, the court makes a winding-up order and appoints a liquidator
13. (c) the powers of the directors' cease, and the liquidator assumes control of the assets of the company

- (d) the liquidator collects debts due to the company, sells its assets and puts the proceeds into a single pot
- (e) the liquidator asks creditors to prove their debts
- (f ) a statement of affairs is prepared, and the liquidator pays corporates debts according to a statutory order set out in section 4 of the study manual, with claims in the same category abating pari passu (being paid x cents per dollar owed)
- (g) final accounts are drawn and submitted to the court with a request for the court to issue of a certificate of dissolution.
6. The usual reasons for a members' voluntary winding-up are that:
- the articles specify that the company shall be wound up upon the expiry of a certain time or on the happening of an event and the event occurs
- the company is no longer needed and the shareholders want their capital to be returned to them.
7. a. Compromise arrangement with creditors in accordance with ss.14-45 of the BVI BCA (per section 1.2 of the course manual)
- b. Administration in accordance with ss.76-114 of the BVI Insolvency Act 2003 as amended (per section 1.3)
- c. Voluntary winding-up in accordance with ss.197-208 of the BVI BCA (per section 3.1)
8. The procedure is described in section 3.1. It involves:
- (a) the directors making a declaration that the company is solvent
- (b) the shareholders passing a (special) resolution that the company be wound-up and appointing a liquidator
- (c) the liquidator collects debts due to the company, sells its assets and puts the proceeds into a single pot
- (d) the liquidator advertises for creditors and asks those creditors to prove their debts
- (e) the liquidator calls a meeting of creditors, unless dispensed with, and pays creditors the amount due to them, and any surplus to the shareholders
- (f ) a final statement of account is drawn and submitted to the registrar with a request to dissolve the company.
9. a. If the creditor has security, they can enforce a fixed or a floating charge by appointing a receiver who will exercise a power of sale over the mortgaged asset (section 1.1).
- b. If the creditor does not have security they have three options. Firstly, although a creditor cannot propose a compromise arrangement, they can induce the board to do so - unlikely in BVI because of the administration procedure described in (c) below (section 1.2).
- c. Secondly, an unsecured creditor can apply to the court for an Administration Order and for the judge to appoint an administrator under Pt 3, ss.76-114 of the BVI Insolvency Act 2003 (per section 1.3).
- d. Thirdly, an unsecured creditor can commence compulsory winding-up proceedings (section 2).

10. The usual grounds for a creditors' voluntary winding-up are that the company is insolvent and the members (and directors) wish to cease business and dissolve the company.
11. The typical procedure for a creditors' voluntary winding-up is described in section 3.2. The main difference to a members' voluntary winding-up is that the creditors select the liquidator at a preliminary meeting of the company's creditors.
12. A liquidator must comply with the duties described in section 2.4, namely:
- to review the affairs of the company and, if necessary, bring a lawsuit against errant directors (in particular those engaged in fraudulent or wrongful trading) in order to 'swell' the assets available to pay the creditors
- to collect debts due to the company and take assets of the company under their control and sell them and convert them to money
- to pay creditors of the company according to the statutory order
- to conclude the winding up by preparing accounts, convene final meetings, and make the necessary filings to formally dissolve the company.
13. The cause is generally failure to make filings, pay annual fees or respond to letters that have been sent by the Registrar of Companies, giving rise to a suspicion that a company is defunct and has ceased to carry on business. The process involves the registrar serving notice of their intention to strike off. If the default is not remedied, the registrar removes the company from the register of companies. Any assets held by the company when struck-off the register automatically vest in the government of the state of incorporation. See section 6.1.
14. A voluntary striking off is less formal, less time consuming and less expensive than a voluntary winding up, which involves the appointment of an independent professional liquidator who must follow statutory procedures. A voluntary striking off is effected by the directors of the company and is only permitted when a company is in a 'zero state' , with no assets and no debts or outstanding liabilities.
15. The procedure is described in section 6.2:
11. (a) the board closes the company's business, liquidates its assets and pays all of its debts
12. (b) when satisfied the company has reached a 'zero-state' the directors apply to the registrar on a prescribed form supported by an affidavit asking for the company to be struck-off
13. (c) the registrar publishes details of the proposed strike-off in the Gazette
14. (d) if no objections are received within a prescribed period, the registrar with strike the company off the register and issues a certificate of voluntary striking off and the company is dissolved.
16. A company that has been compulsorily struck off the register can be reinstated to it upon an application being made by a former director, member or creditor of the company. The procedure may involve an application to the court, which is a judicial process, or an application to the registrar, which is an administrative process. The court or the registrar, depending upon the jurisdiction, will readily reinstate the company provided it pays all its outstanding governmental fees plus interest. See section 6.3.
